Oh God oh no House Davion was right the whole time, the AC 10 really is the ultimate weapon. This one shocked me a bit aswell, as I was completely unprepared for AC10s to be, on average, the best weapon category in the game. The basic AC10 is the second best weapon in this dataset at converting BV into Damage, and all the other variants trade a worse (But still much better than average) ratio for good secondary/special effects. The Ultra AC10 has the chance to just rip it and do 20 damage, and the LBXs are excellent at killing infantry, helicopters, and planes with their cluster shot. LBX and Ultra AC10s also have the same range as an AC5 or PPC, putting them in that longer range belt, which is an advantage. The Base AC/10 is also probably the best weapon to give the Special Autocannon Ammo to, which is really fun and you should use it.

# Takeaways

Overall the general takeaway from this article is that Small Bore Autocannons suck in their base state, with only the Light versions and Rotary versions being good, and they could use a BV reduction at some point if Catalyst is interested in adjusting the BV algorithm. The AC5 is almost a median weapon from a Damage to BV standpoint, and it has good range, but the small hit and opportunity cost of spending 10 tons and a mech chassis to do 5 damage in my opinion holds it back from being decent. Other takeaways are that PPCs have a kind of bad BV to Damage ratio, and are heavy and heat-intensive compared to the AC-10, which is apparently a godly weapon we should all kneel before and pray for salvation.

I was shocked by the PPCs not doing well here, as they had kind of been in the back of my head for the last decade as the “Best” weapons in the game. Large Lasers are very solid and reliable, with basically nothing in that category being super disappointing, my distaste for the IS ER Large Laser aside. If you want to Head Chop someone, you are best off going for a Gauss Rifle rather than a Clan ER PPC, as they cost less BV for similar damage. The ER PPC might be better if it is your first weapon and you can sink it completely with your engine heat sinks as it will be much lighter than a Gauss, and does have the advantage of not blowing up and having unlimited ammo.

My personal picks for the “Best” weapon in the game after looking at all of this would be either of the Large Pulse Lasers, and the AC10s. None of them can head chop, but the AC10 is very cheap and efficient, and the Large Pulse Lasers are super consistent due to their hit bonus cutting through negative hit modifiers from terrain and movement. I personally don’t like counting on Headchopping as a tactic, as in my opinion it doesn’t happen often enough to be worth sacrificing more efficient and reliable damage for, but you can spam enough headchopping weapons to make it work.

None of this math really matters though, because I’ll still get shot in the Center Torso by a “Weak” and “Pathetic” AC2 on the first turn and get TAC’d to the Gyro, but we can at least try to get some optimization out of this horribly unoptimized game.<#sep#>-

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

const int MAXN = 2e5 + 5;
const int LOG = 20;

vector<int> adj[MAXN];
int depth[MAXN], up[LOG][MAXN];

void dfs(int u, int parent) {
    up[0][u] = parent;
    depth[u] = (parent == -1) ? 0 : depth[parent] + 1;
    for (int v : adj[u]) {
        if (v != parent) {
            dfs(v, u);
        }
    }
}

void preprocess(int n) {
    dfs(1, -1);
    for (int j = 1; j < LOG; ++j) {
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; ++i) {
            if (up[j-1][i] == -1) {
                up[j][i] = -1;
            } else {
                up[j][i] = up[j-1][up[j-1][i]];
            }
        }
    }
}

int lca(int u, int v) {
    if (depth[u] < depth[v]) swap(u, v);
    for (int j = LOG-1; j >= 0; --j) {
        if (depth[u] - (1 << j) >= depth[v]) {
            u = up[j][u];
        }
    }
    if (u == v) return u;
    for (int j = LOG-1; j >= 0; --j) {
        if (up[j][u] != up[j][v]) {
            u = up[j][u];
            v = up[j][v];
        }
    }
    return up[0][u];
}

int distance(int u, int v) {
    int ancestor = lca(u, v);
    return depth[u] + depth[v] - 2 * depth[ancestor];
}

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);

    int n;
    cin >> n;
    for (int i = 0; i < n-1; ++i) {
        int x, y;
        cin >> x >> y;
        adj[x].push_back(y);
        adj[y].push_back(x);
    }

    preprocess(n);

    int q;
    cin >> q;
    while (q--) {
        int k, m;
        cin >> k >> m;
        vector<pair<int, int>> viruses(k);
        for (int j = 0; j < k; ++j) {
            cin >> viruses[j].first >> viruses[j].second;
        }
        vector<int> cities(m);
        for (int i = 0; i < m; ++i) {
            cin >> cities[i];
        }

        vector<int> ans;
        for (int u : cities) {
            int best_j = -1;
            long long best_global = LLONG_MAX;
            for (int j = 0; j < k; ++j) {
                int v_j = viruses[j].first;
                int s_j = viruses[j].second;
                int d = distance(u, v_j);
                int steps_j = (d + s_j - 1) / s_j;
                long long global_step = (steps_j - 1) * 1LL * k + (j + 1);
                if (global_step < best_global || (global_step == best_global && (j + 1) < best_j)) {
                    best_global = global_step;
                    best_j = j + 1;
                }
            }
            ans.push_back(best_j);
        }

        for (int i = 0; i < m; ++i) {
            cout << ans[i] << " \n"[i == m-1];
        }
    }

    return 0;
}<#sep#>. Let me analyze the behavior of both sides.

For x approaching infinity: LHS is x^4 -6x³ +30x² -90x +225 ≈ x^4. RHS is 36*cbrt(2x^2 *x^3)=36*cbrt(2x^5)=36*2^(1/3)x^(5/3). As x→∞, x^4 grows much faster than x^(5/3), so LHS > RHS.

At x=3, LHS=144, RHS=144. For x>3, LHS increases rapidly, RHS increases but slower. So maybe no other solution for x>3.

For x between -1 and 3, let's check x=2: LHS=133, RHS≈72*3^(1/3)≈72*1.442≈103.8. LHS > RHS.

At x=1: LHS=160, RHS=0. So LHS > RHS.

At x approaching -1 from the right: x→-1+, then inside cube root approaches 2*(x-1)^2*(x+1)*something positive. As x approaches -1 from the right, x+1 approaches 0+, so inside cube root approaches 0+, so RHS approaches 0. LHS approaches (1 +15)(1 +6 +15)=16*22=352, which is much larger. So LHS > RHS near x=-1.

So between x=-1 and x=3, LHS > RHS everywhere except perhaps where they cross. We have x=3 as a solution. If we check the derivative, maybe see if there's another crossing. But this is getting complex.

</think>To solve the equation:
\[ x^4 - 6x^3 + 30x^2 - 90x + 225 = 36\sqrt[3]{2(x^2-1)(x^3-8x^2+21x-14)}, \]

we start by examining both sides of the equation.

### Step 1: Factor the Polynomials
First, factor the polynomial inside the cube root on the RHS:
\[ x^2 - 1 = (x - 1)(x + 1). \]

Next, factor \( x^3 - 8x^2 + 21x - 14 \):
Using the Rational Root Theorem, we test possible rational roots ±1, ±2, ±7, ±14. We find that \( x = 1 \) is a root:
\[ 1 - 8 + 21 - 14 = 0. \]
Thus, \( x - 1 \) is a factor. Using polynomial division or synthetic division, we get:
\[ x^3 - 8x^2 + 21x - 14 = (x - 1)(x^2 - 7x + 14). \]

So, the RHS becomes:
\[ 36 \sqrt[3]{2(x - 1)^2 (x + 1)(x^2 - 7x + 14)}. \]

### Step 2: Factor the LHS
We factor the LHS polynomial:
\[ x^4 - 6x^3 + 30x^2 - 90x + 225. \]
By trial and error or polynomial factorization techniques, we find:
\[ x^4 - 6x^3 + 30x^2 - 90x + 225 = (x^2 + 15)(x^2 - 6x + 15). \]

### Step 3: Test Potential Solutions
We test \( x = 3 \):
- **LHS Calculation**:
  \[
  3^4 - 6 \cdot 3^3 + 30 \cdot 3^2 - 90 \cdot 3 + 225 = 81 - 162 + 270 - 270 + 225 = 144.
  \]

- **RHS Calculation**:
  \[
  36 \sqrt[3]{2(3^2 - 1)(3^3 - 8 \cdot 3^2 + 21 \cdot 3 - 14)} = 36 \sqrt[3]{2(9 - 1)(27 - 72 + 63 - 14)} = 36 \sqrt[3]{2 \cdot 8 \cdot 4} = 36 \sqrt[3]{64} = 36 \cdot 4 = 144.
  \]

Both sides equal 144 when \( x = 3 \).

### Step 4: Verify Uniqueness of the Solution
We analyze the behavior of both sides:
- The LHS is a quartic polynomial, which grows as \( x^4 \).
- The RHS is \( 36 \sqrt[3]{2(x-1)^2 (x+1)(x^2 - 7x + 14)} \), which grows as \( x^{5/3} \).

For large \( x \), \( x^4 \) grows faster than \( x^{5/3} \), so the LHS will eventually dominate the RHS. For \( x \) near 3, the LHS and RHS are close, and for \( x > 3 \), the LHS increases faster than the RHS. Therefore, \( x = 3 \) is the only real solution.

Thus, the solution to the equation is:

"Roulette pays \$35 to \$1 so the house edge on any winning 35 to 1 bet is 5.26% and the house should win 5.26% of all bets. If player bets and loses \$100, the house doesn't charge then an extra \$5.26 so the edge is only applied when paying off winning bets. If a roulette table has a volume of \$1 million, the house should keep \$52,600 and the winners should collect \$947,400."

Your logic holds for roulette, but not many of the other casino games.  The house edge is applied to all bets placed, not just winning ones.

"If there is an advantage, it's thoroughly understand how to play the game. I won't stay very long at a Blackjack table if the other players are not using basic hitting strategies unless I'm benefiting from their poor play. You might think it's a better game until you see many other players effecting your outcomes."

In Blackjack, the other players decisions do not affect you.  There play is just as likely to hurt or help you, and will even out.  Mathemetically, it doesn't matter to you whether they play basic strategy or not.  If you can't enjoy the game with people playing badly, that's one thing, but know that the math doesn't change.

"Today poker is all about no limit tourneys and the best players in the world can't overcome a run of bad cards. Even the cash games are no limit. How can you grind it out when the other players constantly take you all in?"

It's actually pretty simple.  Don't put down your whole bankroll, just a piece of it.  Use proper bankroll management, and play strong poker against weaker players.  That's how you grind out any advantage, in most games you place bets using the Kelly Criterion (theoretically you could with poker, but you can't actually quantify what you need to know) and in Poker you generally just play with X buy ins, depending on how much risk you are willing to take on.

"You can get the advantage more often playing poker if your good enough, but you still won't win every time. Gambling is about timing and looking for an advantage is the idea of this topic. You can calculate the odds of pocket aces winning hands, but knowing you had the best odds means nothing when the other guy is raking in the pot."

It absolutely means something.  If I got in all in with Aces preflop, I'm over an 80% favorite to win the pot.  That's a great bet!  And to say that it means nothing that you were able to do that if the other player wins is very short sighted.  In Poker, you're going to have bad beats.  If you can list 100 bad beats and not so many times when other players outplayed you then you're in good shape.  The more often you get your money in good, the more often you are likely to win.

Not to mention that there's a million other things that can happen.  In most cases, people don't go all in pre flop and you have a couple more rounds of betting.  A lot of information can be extracted during the flop and turn, which could lead you to fold what WAS the best hand preflop, and has become the 2nd best hand.  You can save A LOT of money by making information bets and making disciplined folds.  And in Poker, each dollar you saved is basically the same as a dollar won.

Think about it, if I was dealt AA and you were dealt KK, and I got you all in, that's good for me.  If the situation's reversed, and I'm able to get away from KK and fold it, then whose that good for?  Obviously you won a small pot, but I was able to get away from a situtation where I'm about 80% to lose my stack.  I'd say that folding KK to AA is a good play for KK.  (Obviously only if he folds it to a really obvious tell, though.  Otherwise he's throwing away KK to any of the premiuum hands, and that's not profitable.)  This situation happened to me ONCE and it's the only time I've ever folded KK preflop.  The other player threw up AA, and everyone else at the table was amazed that I folded.

#include <iostream>
#include <vector>
#include <cmath>
#include <algorithm>

using namespace std;

struct Point {
    double x, y, z;
    Point operator-(const Point& p) const { return {x - p.x, y - p.y, z - p.z}; }
    Point operator+(const Point& p) const { return {x + p.x, y + p.y, z + p.z}; }
    Point operator*(double s) const { return {x * s, y * s, z * s}; }
    double dot(const Point& p) const { return x * p.x + y * p.y + z * p.z; }
    Point cross(const Point& p) const {
        return {y * p.z - z * p.y, z * p.x - x * p.z, x * p.y - y * p.x};
    }
};

struct Plane {
    double a, b, c, d;
    Point normal() const { return {a, b, c}; }
};

Plane compute_plane(const vector<Point>& poly) {
    Point p0 = poly[0], p1 = poly[1], p2 = poly[2];
    Point v1 = p1 - p0, v2 = p2 - p0;
    Point normal = v1.cross(v2);
    double d = -normal.dot(p0);
    return {normal.x, normal.y, normal.z, d};
}

double signed_distance(const Plane& plane, const Point& p) {
    return plane.a * p.x + plane.b * p.y + plane.c * p.z + plane.d;
}

Point line_plane_intersection(const Point& p1, const Point& p2, const Plane& plane) {
    double d1 = signed_distance(plane, p1);
    double d2 = signed_distance(plane, p2);
    double t = d1 / (d1 - d2);
    return p1 + (p2 - p1) * t;
}

// Project 3D point to 2D using plane's basis vectors
void project_to_2d(const vector<Point>& poly, const Plane& plane, Point p, double& u, double& v) {
    Point basis_u, basis_v;
    Point normal = plane.normal();
    
    // Find a non-parallel vector to normal to use as basis_u
    if (abs(normal.x) > 1e-6 || abs(normal.y) > 1e-6)
        basis_u = normal.cross({0, 0, 1});
    else
        basis_u = normal.cross({1, 0, 0});
    basis_u = basis_u * (1.0 / sqrt(basis_u.dot(basis_u))); // normalize
    
    basis_v = normal.cross(basis_u);
    basis_v = basis_v * (1.0 / sqrt(basis_v.dot(basis_v))); // normalize
    
    Point origin = poly[0];
    Point vec = p - origin;
    u = vec.dot(basis_u);
    v = vec.dot(basis_v);
}

bool is_point_inside(const vector<Point>& poly, const Plane& plane, const Point& p) {
    // Project the polygon and point to 2D
    vector<pair<double, double>> poly2d;
    double pu, pv;
    project_to_2d(poly, plane, p, pu, pv);
    for (const auto& pt : poly) {
        double u, v;
        project_to_2d(poly, plane, pt, u, v);
        poly2d.emplace_back(u, v);
    }
    
    // Ray casting algorithm
    int n = poly2d.size();
    bool inside = false;
    for (int i = 0, j = n-1; i < n; j = i++) {
        double xi = poly2d[i].first, yi = poly2d[i].second;
        double xj = poly2d[j].first, yj = poly2d[j].second;
        
        bool intersect = ((yi > pv) != (yj > pv)) && 
            (pu < (xj - xi) * (pv - yi) / (yj - yi) + xi);
        if (intersect)
            inside = !inside;
    }
    return inside;
}

int check_polygon(const vector<Point>& polyA, const Plane& planeA, const vector<Point>& polyB) {
    int cnt[2] = {0, 0};
    for (size_t i = 0; i < polyB.size(); ++i) {
        const Point& p1 = polyB[i];
        const Point& p2 = polyB[(i+1)%polyB.size()];
        double d1 = signed_distance(planeA, p1);
        double d2 = signed_distance(planeA, p2);
        if (d1 * d2 > 1e-9) continue; // same side
        
        Point ip = line_plane_intersection(p1, p2, planeA);
        if (!is_point_inside(polyA, planeA, ip)) continue;
        
        // Determine direction: positive to negative or vice versa
        int dir = (d1 > 0 || d2 > 0) ? 0 : 1;
        if (d1 > 0 || d2 > 0) {
            if (d1 > 0 && d2 < 0) dir = 0;
            else dir = 1;
        } else {
            if (d1 < 0 && d2 > 0) dir = 1;
            else dir = 0;
        }
        cnt[dir]++;
    }
    return (cnt[0] - cnt[1]) % 2 != 0;
}

int main() {
    ios::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);
    
    int n, m;
    vector<Point> polyA, polyB;
    
    cin >> n;
    polyA.resize(n);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) 
        cin >> polyA[i].x >> polyA[i].y >> polyA[i].z;
    
    cin >> m;
    polyB.resize(m);
    for (int i = 0; i < m; ++i)
        cin >> polyB[i].x >> polyB[i].y >> polyB[i].z;
    
    Plane planeA = compute_plane(polyA);
    Plane planeB = compute_plane(polyB);
    
    bool resA = check_polygon(polyA, planeA, polyB);
    bool resB = check_polygon(polyB, planeB, polyA);
    
    if ((resA || resB))
        cout << "YES\n";
    else
        cout << "NO\n";
    
    return 0;

\end{matrix}
\right.
\]

\]

Hmm, all three equations look kind of similar. Each one has a variable squared multiplied by another variable plus three, and it's equal to 4 times (2 minus another variable). x²(x + 3) = 4(2 - x)

Let me write that out:

x²(x + 3) = 8 - 4x

Let's expand the left side:

x³ + 3x² = 8 - 4x

Bring all terms to one side:

x³ + 3x² + 4x - 8 = 0

Now I need to solve this cubic equation. Maybe I can try rational roots. The possible rational roots are factors of 8 over factors of 1, so ±1, ±2, ±4, ±8.

Testing x=1: 1 + 3 + 4 -8 = 0. Oh! 1 + 3 is 4, plus 4 is 8, minus 8 is 0. So x=1 is a root.

Therefore, (x - 1) is a factor. Let's perform polynomial division or use synthetic division.

Dividing x³ + 3x² + 4x -8 by (x - 1). Using synthetic division:

Coefficients: 1 | 3 | 4 | -8

Bring down the 1. Multiply by 1: 1. Add to next coefficient: 3 + 1 = 4. Multiply by 1: 4. Add to next coefficient: 4 + 4 = 8. Multiply by 1: 8. Add to last coefficient: -8 +8 =0.

So the polynomial factors as (x - 1)(x² + 4x +8) = 0.

Setting each factor to zero:

x -1 = 0 → x=1

x² +4x +8 =0. The discriminant is 16 -32 = -16, so complex roots. Since we are probably looking for real solutions, the only real solution here is x=1.

Now, code-wise:

All steps per bot per tick:

1. Determine current target (nearest enemy)

2. Update target via TARGET command.

3. Generate Move command towards target's current position.

4. Send Power command based on distance to target.

The main challenge is implementing these steps with the available API.

Now, let's draft a Python example.

The example programs are provided by the problem's host (probably in C++ or similar), but to make a simple player I'll try to outline steps.

First, connect to the server:

import socket
import json
import struct
from math import sqrt

# Create a socket and connect to the game engine, which is on the same machine at port 50000

s = socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_STREAM)
s.connect(('localhost', 50000))

# Receive messages as newline-terminated JSON strings

def recv_json(s):
    data = b''
    while True:
        chunk = s.recv(1)
        if not chunk:
            return None
        if chunk == b'\n':
            break
        data += chunk
    return json.loads(data.decode('utf-8'))

def send_json(s, data):
    req = json.dumps(data) + '\n'
    s.sendall( req.encode('utf-8') )

# Handle the game loop.

# state variables:

bots = {}  # key is (pid, bid), value is {x,y, ...}

my_PID = None
our_bots = []  # list of (bid, ...) 

# Receive the "Ready" message first.

msg = recv_json(s)
while msg and msg['Type'] == 'BOT':
    # Wait until we get the READY first.
    pass  # wait, no: expect before the READY, all initial data?

Wait, the problem says:

When the game is launched, it will begin listening on port... Once two player programs connect, it sends READY to them.

Wait so the first thing each program receives is the READY, then the subsequent messages are BOT updates.

Wait: the READY message is only sent once, and gives the initial list of Bots for all players.

Therefore, upon connecting, the first message received is READY.

So in code:

def main():
    s = connect...

    # receive READY first:
    ready_msg = recv_json(s)
    
    if ready_msg.get('Type') != 'READY':
        print("Expected READY, but got something else.")
    
    my_PID = ready_msg['PID']
    # build initial bots:
    for bot_d in ready_msg["Bots"]:
        pid = bot_d['PID']
        bid = bot_d['BID']
        key = (pid, bid)
        bots[key] = bot_d.copy()
        # also track our bots:
        if pid == my_PID:
            our_bots.append( (bid, bot_d) )

    print(f"Ready as player {my_PID}, with {len(our_bots)} bots")

    # Now, enter main game loop.

    # While game is running:

    while True:

        # Wait for any incoming network messages
        # So it can be an incoming message (BOT, etc.)
        #
        # Alternatively, since in non-blocking may complicate, just process as messages come in.
        #
        # We could process the current state each tick, but in the TCP:

        # We need to handle incoming messages first. Let's read all that arrived until now.

        # This approach may not work properly since in a single thread, but for code outline:

        # While there are incoming messages, process them.

        msg = recv_json(s)
        while msg:
            typ = msg['Type']
            if typ == 'BOT':
                pid = msg['PID']
                bid = msg['BID']
                key = (pid, bid)
                bots[key].update( msg )
            # else: ignore other types
            msg = recv_json(s)

        # Now, at this point, our bots and enemies are known, based on current messages.

        # For each of our bots, decide actions for this tick.

        for bid, our_bot in our_bots:

            # Find all enemy bots (PID != my_PID where status is alive?)

            enemy_bots = []
            for (other_pid, other_bid), bot in bots.items():
                if other_pid != my_PID and bot.get('Health',0) >0:
                    enemy_bots.append( bot.copy() )

            if not enemy_bots:
                # no enemies? Then maybe stop? Not our concern for first version.
                continue

            # Find closest enemy bot:

            my_x = our_bot['X']
            my_y = our_bot['Y']

            closest = None
            closest_dist_sq = float('inf')

            for enemy in enemy_bots:
                enemy_x = enemy['X']
                enemy_y = enemy['Y']

                dx = enemy_x - my_x
                dy = enemy_y - my_y

                dist_sq = dx*dx + dy*dy

                if dist_sq < closest_dist_sq:
                    closest = enemy
                    closest_dist_sq = dist_sq

            if closest is None:
                continue  # ?

            closest_dist = sqrt(closest_dist_sq)

            # Now, this bot's actions:

            # Step1: target this closest bot

            # need to send a Target message.

            target_pid = closest['PID']
            target_bid = closest['BID']

            # Target command is on this bot (BID=bid)

            target_cmd = {
                "Cmd":"TARGET",
                "BID": bid,
                "TPID": target_pid,
                "TBID": target_bid
            }

            send_json(s, target_cmd)

            # Step2: move toward the enemy's current position (closest's X/Y).

            move_x = closest['X']
            move_y = closest['Y']

            move_cmd = {
                "Cmd":"MOVE",
                "BID":bid,
                "X": move_x,
                "Y": move_y 
            }
            send_json(s, move_cmd)

            # Step3: decide Power allocation based on distance.

            if closest_dist > 600:
                # allocate towards moving:
                MPow=6
                FPow=4
                SPow=2
            else:

The forecasts are initialised on the first of each month with a 24-hourly time step, and cover 123 days.\nCompanion datasets, also available through the Climate Data Store (CDS), are the operational forecasts, historical simulations that can be used to derive the hydrological climatology, and medium-range and seasonal reforecasts. The latter dataset enables research, local skill assessment and post-processing of the seasonal forecasts. It is accompanied by an ancillary file for interpretation that provides the upstream area (see the related variables table and associated link in the documentation).\nThis dataset was produced by forcing a hydrological modelling chain with input from the European Centre for Medium-range Weather Forecasts (ECMWF) ensemble seasonal forecasting system, SEAS5.  GRIDDED_GLACIERS_MASS_CHANGE,"The dataset provides annual glacier mass changes distributed on a global regular grid at 0.5° resolution (latitude, longitude). Glaciers play a fundamental role in the Earth's water cycles. They are one of the most important freshwater resources for societies and ecosystems and the recent increase in ice melt contributes directly to the rise of ocean levels. Due to this they have been declared as an Essential Climate Variable (ECV) by GCOS, the Global Climate Observing System. Within the Copernicus Services, the global gridded annual glacier mass change dataset provides information on changing glacier resources by combining glacier change observations from the Fluctuations of Glaciers (FoG) database that is brokered from World Glacier Monitoring Service (WGMS). Previous glacier products were provided to the Copernicus Climate Change Service (C3S) Climate Data Store (CDS) as a homogenized state-of-the-art glacier dataset with separated elevation and mass change time series collected by scientists and the national correspondents of each country as provided to the WGMS (see Related data). The new approach combines glacier mass balances from in-situ observations with glacier elevation changes from remote sensing to generate a new gridded product of annual glacier mass changes and related uncertainties for every hydrological year since 1975/76 provided in a 0.5°x0.5° global regular grid. The dataset bridges the gap on spatio-temporal coverage of glacier change observations, providing for the first time in the CDS an annually resolved glacier mass change product using the glacier elevation change sample as calibration. This goal has become feasible at the global scale thanks to a new globally near-complete (96 percent of the world glaciers) dataset of glacier elevation change observations recently ingested by the FoG database. To develop the distributed glacier change product the glacier outlines were used from the Randolph Glacier Inventory 6.0 (see Related data). A glacier is considered to belong to a grid-point when its geometric centroid lies within the grid point. The centroid is obtained from the glacier outlines from the Randolph Glacier Inventory 6.0. The glacier mass changes in the unit Gigatonnes (1 Gt = 1x10^9 tonnes) correspond to the total mass of water lost/gained over the glacier surface during a given year. Note that to propagate to mm/cm/m of water column on the grid cell, the grid cell area needs to be considered. Also note that the data is provided for hydrological years, which vary between the Northern Hemisphere (01 October to 30 September next year) and the Southern Hemisphere (01 April to 31 March next year). This dataset has been produced by researchers at the WGMS on behalf of Cop<#sep#>36-38

#include <iostream>
#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <map>
#include <algorithm>
using namespace std;

class WidgetBase {
public:
    virtual ~WidgetBase() = default;
    virtual pair<int, int> computeSize() const = 0;
    string name;
    WidgetBase(const string& n) : name(n) {}
};

class SimpleWidget : public WidgetBase {
    int w, h;
public:
    SimpleWidget(const string& n, int w, int h) : WidgetBase(n), w(w), h(h) {}
    pair<int, int> computeSize() const override {
        return {w, h};
    }
};

class HBoxWidget : public WidgetBase {
public:
    vector<WidgetBase*> children;
    int border = 0;
    int spacing = 0;

    HBoxWidget(const string& n) : WidgetBase(n) {}

    pair<int, int> computeSize() const override {
        if (children.empty()) return {0, 0};
        int total_width = 0, max_height = 0;
        for (size_t i = 0; i < children.size(); ++i) {
            auto [cw, ch] = children[i]->computeSize();
            total_width += cw + (i ? spacing : 0);
            max_height = max(max_height, ch);
        }
        return {total_width + 2 * border, max_height + 2 * border};
    }
};

class VBoxWidget : public WidgetBase {
public:
    vector<WidgetBase*> children;
    int border = 0;
    int spacing = 0;

    VBoxWidget(const string& n) : WidgetBase(n) {}

    pair<int, int> computeSize() const override {
        if (children.empty()) return {0, 0};
        int total_height = 0, max_width = 0;
        for (size_t i = 0; i < children.size(); ++i) {
            auto [cw, ch] = children[i]->computeSize();
            total_height += ch + (i ? spacing : 0);
            max_width = max(max_width, cw);
        }
        return {max_width + 2 * border, total_height + 2 * border};
    }
};

int main() {
    map<string, WidgetBase*> widgets;
    int n;
    cin >> n;
    cin.ignore();

    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
        string line;
        getline(cin, line);

        if (line.substr(0, 6) == "Widget") {
            size_t p = line.find('(');
            string name = line.substr(7, p - 7);
            string args = line.substr(p + 1, line.size() - p - 2);
            size_t c = args.find(',');
            int x = stoi(args.substr(0, c)), y = stoi(args.substr(c + 1));
            widgets[name] = new SimpleWidget(name, x, y);
        } else if (line.substr(0, 4) == "HBox") {
            widgets[line.substr(5)] = new HBoxWidget(line.substr(5));
        } else if (line.substr(0, 4) == "VBox") {
            widgets[line.substr(5)] = new VBoxWidget(line.substr(5));
        } else {
            size_t dot = line.find('.');
            string name = line.substr(0, dot);
            string rest = line.substr(dot + 1);
            size_t paren = rest.find('(');
            string method = rest.substr(0, paren);
            string arg = rest.substr(paren + 1, rest.size() - paren - 2);

            WidgetBase* obj = widgets[name];
            if (method == "pack") {
                WidgetBase* child = widgets[arg];
                if (auto h = dynamic_cast<HBoxWidget*>(obj)) h->children.push_back(child);
                else if (auto v = dynamic_cast<VBoxWidget*>(obj)) v->children.push_back(child);
            } else if (method == "set_border") {
                int val = stoi(arg);
                if (auto h = dynamic_cast<HBoxWidget*>(obj)) h->border = val;
                else if (auto v = dynamic_cast<VBoxWidget*>(obj)) v->border = val;
            } else if (method == "set_spacing") {
                int val = stoi(arg);
                if (auto h = dynamic_cast<HBoxWidget*>(obj)) h->spacing = val;
                else if (auto v = dynamic_cast<VBoxWidget*>(obj)) v->spacing = val;
            }
        }
    }

    vector<string> names;
    for (auto& [k, v] : widgets) names.push_back(k);
    sort(names.begin(), names.end());

    for (auto& name : names) {
        auto [w, h] = widgets[name]->computeSize();
        cout << name << " " << w << " " << h << "\n";
    }

    for (auto& [k, v] : widgets) delete v;
    return 0;

But since the equations are cyclic, but not symmetric in variables. Wait, the system is cyclic but not fully symmetric. Because each equation relates two variables in a cycle. So solving such a system would require more complex substitution.

Alternatively, consider that each equation is of the form a²(b +3) =4(2 -b). Let's analyze one equation in general.

Suppose we have variables a and b, such that a²(b +3) =4(2 -b). Then we can express b in terms of a as:

a²b +3a² =8 -4b

Bring terms with b to one side:

a²b +4b =8 -3a²

Factor b:

b(a² +4)=8 -3a²

Thus:

b=(8 -3a²)/(a² +4)

So as I derived earlier.

Therefore, given x, y, z, each can be expressed in terms of the previous variable.

So starting with x, we can compute y from x, then z from y, then x from z, and check if the cycle closes.

So in other words, starting with x, we have:

y = (8 -3x²)/(x² +4)

z = (8 -3y²)/(y² +4)

x' = (8 -3z²)/(z² +4)

And the system is consistent only if x' = x.

Therefore, the problem reduces to finding fixed points of the function f(f(f(x))) = x, where f(a) = (8 -3a²)/(a² +4)

This seems challenging, but maybe we can look for fixed points where f(x) =x, so if x is a fixed point, then f(f(f(x)))=x would hold.

So let's first check if there are fixed points of f(x).

Set f(x) =x:

(8 -3x²)/(x² +4) =x

Multiply both sides by (x² +4):

8 -3x² =x(x² +4)

8 -3x² =x³ +4x

Bring all terms to left:

x³ +4x +3x² -8 =0

x³ +3x² +4x -8=0

Wait, this is the same cubic equation as before! So x³ +3x² +4x -8=0. Which factors as (x -1)(x² +4x +8)=0. So fixed points of f(x) are x=1 and complex roots. Therefore, the only real fixed point is x=1. Therefore, the only real solution where f(x)=x is x=1, leading to y=1, z=1.

y = f(x)

z = f(y)

x = f(z)

#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <unordered_map>
#include <algorithm>
#include <cstring>

using namespace std;

int main() {
    string s;
    cin >> s;
    int n = s.size();

    // Standard sliding window to compute max_len
    int max_len = 0;
    vector<int> last_occurrence(20, -1);
    int start = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        char c = s[i] - 'a';
        if (last_occurrence[c] >= start) {
            start = last_occurrence[c] + 1;
        }
        last_occurrence[c] = i;
        max_len = max(max_len, i - start + 1);
    }

    // Compute prefix masks and lengths
    vector<int> left_masks(n, 0);
    vector<int> left_length(n, 0);
    last_occurrence.assign(20, -1);
    start = 0;
    int current_mask = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        char c = s[i] - 'a';
        if (last_occurrence[c] >= start) {
            while (start <= last_occurrence[c]) {
                char d = s[start] - 'a';
                current_mask &= ~(1 << d);
                start++;
            }
        }
        current_mask |= (1 << c);
        left_masks[i] = current_mask;
        left_length[i] = i - start + 1;
        last_occurrence[c] = i;
    }

    // Compute suffix masks and lengths
    vector<int> right_masks(n, 0);
    vector<int> right_length(n, 0);
    last_occurrence.assign(20, n);
    int end = n - 1;
    current_mask = 0;
    for (int i = n - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
        char c = s[i] - 'a';
        if (last_occurrence[c] <= end) {
            while (end >= last_occurrence[c]) {
                char d = s[end] - 'a';
                current_mask &= ~(1 << d);
                end--;
            }
        }
        current_mask |= (1 << c);
        right_masks[i] = current_mask;
        right_length[i] = end - i + 1;
        last_occurrence[c] = i;
    }

    // Build left_map and right_map
    unordered_map<int, int> left_map;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        int mask = left_masks[i];
        if (left_map.find(mask) == left_map.end() || left_map[mask] < left_length[i]) {
            left_map[mask] = left_length[i];
        }
    }

    unordered_map<int, int> right_map;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        int mask = right_masks[i];
        if (right_map.find(mask) == right_map.end() || right_map[mask] < right_length[i]) {
            right_map[mask] = right_length[i];
        }
    }

    // Precompute max_right
    int max_right[1 << 20] = {0};
    for (auto& p : right_map) {
        int mask = p.first;
        int len = p.second;
        max_right[mask] = max(max_right[mask], len);
    }

    // Dynamic programming to propagate max_right to all subsets
    for (int mask = (1 << 20) - 1; mask >= 0; mask--) {
        for (int bit = 0; bit < 20; bit++) {
            if (mask & (1 << bit)) {
                int sub_mask = mask ^ (1 << bit);
                max_right[sub_mask] = max(max_right[sub_mask], max_right[mask]);
            }
        }
    }

    // Compute the maximum sum of left and right parts
    int max_sum = 0;
    for (auto& p : left_map) {
        int left_mask = p.first;
        int left_len = p.second;
        int allowed_mask = ((1 << 20) - 1) ^ left_mask;
        max_sum = max(max_sum, left_len + max_right[allowed_mask]);
    }

    int result = max(max_len, max_sum);
    cout << result << endl;

    return 0;

Alternatively, consider the function f(a) = (8 -3a²)/(a² +4). We can analyze its behavior to see if cycles are possible.

Compute derivative of f(a):

f(a) = (8 -3a²)/(a² +4)

Using quotient rule:

f’(a)= [ (-6a)(a² +4) - (8 -3a²)(2a) ] / (a² +4)^2

Simplify numerator:

-6a(a² +4) -2a(8 -3a²)

= -6a³ -24a -16a +6a³

= (-6a³ +6a³) + (-24a -16a) = -40a

Thus, f’(a)= -40a / (a² +4)^2

At a=1, f’(1)= -40(1)/ (1 +4)^2= -40/25= -1.6. The magnitude is greater than 1, so fixed point at a=1 is unstable.

Therefore, iteration of f may diverge from 1, unless started exactly there. So, given that the derivative at x=1 is -1.6, which magnitude is greater than 1, this fixed point is a repeller. So other cycles might exist, but given the derivative of the composition would have a derivative product of (-1.6)^3 ≈-4.096, which also has magnitude greater than 1. Therefore, such cycles would also be unstable, but since we are looking for real solutions, maybe they exist but are not attracting, hence hard to find via iteration.

Alternatively, maybe there are no other real solutions. Given that the only real fixed point is 1 and attempts to find other solutions through substitution lead to inconsistencies or complex numbers, it's possible that x=y=z=1 is the only real solution.

Therefore, after going through these possibilities and not finding any other real solutions, I think the only real solution is x=1, y=1, z=1. So the boxed answer is each variable equal to 1.

The solution to the system is \boxed{x = 1}, \boxed{y = 1}, and \boxed{z = 1}.

But the<#sep#>Another thing: in the move command, the destination is the current position of the target (as per last known info). 

Wait, in the code above, for the enemy's X/Y, we take the current value from the bots dict, which is updated as messages arrive.

Thus, the destination is the latest known coordinate of the target.

The Move command then issues the bot moving towards that position.

Thus, even if the enemy moves next tick, the bot aims at where they were at previous tick.

Thus maybe it's suboptimal, but the most we can do given info.

Enemy bots spawn roughly 1440du apart.

The bots need to reach each other.

The Power allocations need to be chosen such that when they are close, firepower is boosted.

Additionally, during the movement phase, the fire allocation of 4 would give 4 shots per 12 ticks (so every 3 seconds?), but with longer distance, so hitting less often, but the beam's damage at longer distance is lower.

However, while approaching, the damage accumulates.

This is the hope.

The main goal is to make sure that the bots are alive long enough to inflict more damage than the enemy.

Here's the basic structure in a Python script:

[The following code is a conceptual outline and may require testing and adjustment]

---

import socket
import json
import math

HOST = "localhost"
PORT = 50000

class BotPlayer:
    def __init__(self):
        self.sock = socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_STREAM)
        self.sock.connect((HOST, PORT))
        self.bots = {}  # (pid, bid) → dict of bot data
        self.my_pid = None
        self.my_bots = []  # list of (bid, ...) for our bots
        self.target_cache = {}  # bid → (tpid, tbid)
        self.power_cache = {}  # bid → (mpow, fpow, spow)

    def run(self):
        self._handle_ready()
        while True:
            self._process_messages()
            actions = self._calculate_actions()
            for action in actions:
                self._send_command(action)
    
    def _handle_ready(self):
        msg = self._recv_json()
        if msg['Type'] != 'READY':
            raise RuntimeError("Expected READY")
        self.my_pid = msg['PID']
        for bot in msg['Bots']:
            pid = bot['PID']
            bid = bot['BID']
            self.bots[(pid, bid)] = bot
            if pid == self.my_pid:
                self.my_bots.append(bid)
        self.power_cache = {bid: (4,4,4) for bid in self.my_bots}
        self.target_cache = {bid: (0,0) for bid in self.my_bots}

    def _process_messages(self):
        while True:
            try:
                msg = self._recv_json()
                if not msg:
                    break
                if msg['Type'] == 'BOT':
                    self._update_bot(msg['PID'], msg['BID'], msg)
            except BlockingIOError:
                break  # no more messages
    
    def _update_bot(self, pid, bid, data):
        key = (pid, bid)
        self.bots.get(key, {}).update(data)
    
    def _recv_json(self):
        chunks = []
        while True:
            chunk = self.sock.recv(1)
            if not chunk:
                return None
            if chunk == b'\n':
                break
            chunks.append(chunk)
        raw = b''.join(chunks).decode('utf-8')
        return json.loads(raw)
    
    def _send_command(self, cmd):
        self.sock.sendall( (json.dumps(cmd) + '\n').encode('utf-8') )
    
    def _calculate_actions(self):
        actions = []
        for bid in self.my_bots:
            bot_data = self._get_bot(self.my_pid, bid)
            if not bot_data:
                continue  # bot is dead
            # Find closest enemy
            enemies = [b for (pid, b_id), b in self.bots.items()
                       if pid != self.my_pid and b['Health'] >0]
            if not enemies:
                continue
            closest_bid, closest = self._find_closest(bot_data, enemies)
            # compute commands for this bid
            curr_target = self.target_cache.get(bid, (None, None))
            new_target = (closest['PID'], closest['BID'])
            if new_target != curr_target:
                target_cmd = {
                    'Cmd': 'TARGET',
                    'BID': bid,
                    'TPID': closest['PID'],
                    'TBID': closest['BID']
                }
                actions.append(target_cmd)
                self.target_cache[bid] = new_target
            
            # Calculate move to target's position
            move_x = closest['X']
            move_y = closest['Y']
            move_cmd = {
                'Cmd': 'MOVE',
                'BID': bid,
                'X': move_x,
                'Y': move_y
            }
            actions.append(move_cmd)
            
            # determine power allocation
            distance_sq = (closest['X'] - bot_data['X'])**2 + (closest['Y'] - bot_data['Y'])**2
            distance = math.sqrt(distance_sq)
            if distance > 600:
                mpow, fpow, spow = 6,4,2
            else:
                mpow, fpow, spow = 2,8,2
            
            current_pow = self.power_cache.get(bid, (0,0,0))
            if (mpow, fpow, spow) != current_pow:
                power_cmd = {
                    'Cmd': 'POWER',
                    'BID': bid,
                    'MPow': mpow,
                    'FPow': fpow,
                    'SPow': spow
                }
                actions.append(power_cmd)
                self.power_cache[bid] = (mpow, fpow, spow)
        return actions
    
    def _find_closest(self, bot, enemies):
        min_dist_sq = float('inf')
        closest = None
        bot_x, bot_y = bot['X'], bot['Y']
        for enemy in enemies:
            dx = enemy['X'] - bot_x
            dy = enemy['Y'] - bot_y
            dist_sq = dx*dx + dy*dy
            if dist_sq < min_dist_sq:
                min_dist_sq = dist_sq
                closest = enemy
        return closest
    
    def _get_bot(self, pid, bid):
        return self.bots.get((pid, bid), None)

if __name__ == '__main__':
    player = BotPlayer()
    player.run()

1 A METHOD OF CALIBRATING HELMHOLTZ COILS FOR THE MEASUREMENT OF PERMANENT MAGNETS Joseph J. Stupak Jr, Oersted Technology Tualatin, Oregon (reprinted from IMCSD 24th Annual Proceedings 1995) ABSTRACT The Helmholtz coil configuration is often used to generate a uniform magnetic field in space, and may also be used to measure the magnetic moment of bar and plate magnets. In the past, Helmholtz coils used for measurement have been calibrated by the use of standard magnets. A method is presented here for calibrating these coils using a known current source and gaussmeter, which should be easier and result in greater accuracy. 1. INTRODUCTION Manufacturers and users of permanent magnets need to measure the strengths of large numbers of magnets by means which are quick, accurate, and require a minimum of calculation and labor. Many (but not all) magnets may be measured in this way by the use of a Helmholtz coil together with an integrating fluxmeter. Helmholtz coils used in this manner in the past have usually been calibrated by use of standard magnets. Since permanent magnet materials are not perfectly homogeneous in their magnetic properties (and may vary with temperature and age, as well), and since NIST (the former National Bureau of Standards) does not maintain magnetic standards, such magnets may be difficult to obtain or confirm to sufficient accuracy. The method suggested in this paper is an indirect one, but is easily accomplished and enables coil calibration by means which may be known to high accuracy. A Helmholtz coil is actually a pair of similar coils with equal numbers of turns. The coils are short, thin-walled rings, and are mounted coaxially at a distance of one coil radius from each other. When the coils are connected (preferably in series) and current is passed through them, a highly uniform magnetic field is produced in a considerable volume of space between the two coils. At the center, the first three derivatives of field strength with position all go to zero in every direction (reference 2). It is this property, plus the fact that the region of uniform field is accessible from almost every direction, which gives the Fig.1: Helmholtz Coil

2 Helmholtz construction its special usefulness. The type of magnet measurable by a Helmholtz coil is one which can be represented by a magnet dipole, or assembly of dipoles with a common axial direction. A magnetic dipole, as shown in figure 2, consists of two magnetic poles of equal and opposite strength, separated by a distance l p. The North pole is a magnetic flux source, radiating straight lines of flux outward with equal distribution in all direction. The opposite South pole is a flux sink, into which flux disappears at the same density per solid angle, from all direction. Since the surface of a sphere is equal to 4πr 2, a single magnetic pole of strength 4π produces a unit magnetic field at unit radius. The magnetic moment of the dipole is equal to the pole strength (in units of flux density times area, or flux) times the pole spacing l p. For a real magnet, this is equal to the integral of flux density times a differential of volume, over the entire volume of the magnet. Long, thin magnets are reasonably well represented by a magnetic dipole, except close to the magnet, and plate magnets may be approximated by a series of dipoles with a common direction. With suitable corrections, arc segments may also be represented in this way (reference 1). Cylindrical magnets, such as those used in some brushless DC motors and rotary linear actuators, cannot be measured by this technique because the magnetic axes do not lie in the same direction, and cancel partially or completely. When a Helmholtz assembly is used to measure a magnet, the magnet is placed between the coils in the region of uniform field, with the magnetic axis parallel to the coil axis, and the fluxmeter attached to the coils is zeroed. The magnet is then withdrawn far enough from the coils that its effect on them is negligible. The reading on the fluxmeter times the coil constant is then equal to the magnetic moment of the tested part. Another method is to rotate the magnet

3 180. The fluxmeter reading times the coil constant is then equal to twice the magnetic moment of the part. If magnetic flux Φ linking an electrically conductive coil of n turns changes with time, a voltage is developed across the coil according to the Faraday induction law, The fluxmeter reading is proportional to the integral of voltage with time, In the above, the constant k m is a scale multiplier which may be set on the instrument, and the constant C is the offset, normally set to zero before the reading is made. Fluxmeters are often used together with a search coil of known area and number of turns, to determine the average magnetic flux density component normal to the plane of the coil, as an averaging gaussmeter. They may also be used to determine the total magnetic flux crossing a section within a magnet or magnetically permeable pole structure, at locations which would not be accessible to a Hall-type gaussmeter. For this purpose a close-fitting coil is wrapped around the part and connected to the fluxmeter, and the instrument is then zeroed. The coil is then removed from the part, without disconnecting it from the fluxmeter, and taken far enough that the magnetic circuit has no noticeable effect on the meter. The fluxmeter reading is then proportional to the total flux crossing the section. It is important that the coil be as closely fitting as possible, to avoid errors due to flux leakage. A correction is sometimes applied for the sense wire thickness. When measurements are made with a Helmholtz coil, on the other hand, there is free space around the part, and handling is much easier. By comparing the units of magnetic moment (magnetic flux density times length cubed) to those of the fluxmeter output (magnetic flux density times length squared), it is apparent that the Helmholtz coil calibration constant will include a unit of length. If the coil windings have negligibly small winding thickness and axial length and if the coils are perfectly constructed and aligned, the coil constant may be derived from its dimensions and number of turns. If the device is to have a coil constant large enough to be useful without excessive resistance, however, the winding dimensions will probably be large enough to affect the measurement accuracy. It is possible to correct the calculation to account for these dimensions (reference 2). Other errors, however, are more difficult to handle, such as misalignment, variations in winding, coil spacing errors, out-of roundness of the coils, etc. Because of these, it is best to measure the coil constant by some means, such as the use of standard magnets or the method described here.

Magnetic flux density may be measured by use of a gaussmeter. Some gaussmeters (such as those based on nuclear magnetic resonance) are capable of excellent accuracy. Another possibility would be to measure the flux crossing a coil of known area and turns by use of the same fluxmeter used for the moment measurements, thus canceling any scale error which might be present. Measurements at our lab, made with instruments of somewhat limited accuracy, confirm equations to an accuracy of about.5% with a total uncertainty of measurement from all sources of about.7% maximum. #include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

vector<int> find_cycle(int N, const vector<vector<int>>& adj) {
    vector<int> parent(N, -1);
    vector<int> visited(N, 0);
    vector<int> cycle;
    int cycle_start = -1;

    function<bool(int, int)> dfs = [&](int u, int prev) {
        visited[u] = 1;
        for (int v : adj[u]) {
            if (v == prev) continue;
            if (visited[v] == 1) {
                cycle_start = v;
                cycle.push_back(v);
                int cur = u;
                while (cur != cycle_start) {
                    cycle.push_back(cur);
                    cur = parent[cur];
                }
                reverse(cycle.begin(), cycle.end());
                return true;
            } else if (visited[v] == 0) {
                parent[v] = u;
                if (dfs(v, u)) {
                    return true;
                }
            }
        }
        visited[u] = 2;
        return false;
    };

    for (int i = 0; i < N; ++i) {
        if (visited[i] == 0 && dfs(i, -1)) {
            break;
        }
    }

    return cycle;
}

int bfs_farthest(int start, const vector<vector<int>>& adj, const set<pair<int, int>>& forbidden) {
    int N = adj.size();
    vector<int> dist(N, -1);
    queue<int> q;
    q.push(start);
    dist[start] = 0;
    int far_node = start;

    while (!q.empty()) {
        int u = q.front();
        q.pop();
        for (int v : adj[u]) {
            if (dist[v] == -1 && !forbidden.count({u, v})) {
                dist[v] = dist[u] + 1;
                q.push(v);
                if (dist[v] > dist[far_node]) {
                    far_node = v;
                }
            }
        }
    }

    return far_node;
}

int bfs_sum(int start, const vector<vector<int>>& adj, const set<pair<int, int>>& forbidden) {
    int N = adj.size();
    vector<int> dist(N, -1);
    queue<int> q;
    q.push(start);
    dist[start] = 0;
    int sum = 0;

    while (!q.empty()) {
        int u = q.front();
        q.pop();
        sum += dist[u];
        for (int v : adj[u]) {
            if (dist[v] == -1 && !forbidden.count({u, v})) {
                dist[v] = dist[u] + 1;
                q.push(v);
            }
        }
    }

    return sum;
}

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(nullptr);

    int N;
    cin >> N;
    vector<vector<int>> adj(N);
    for (int i = 0; i < N; ++i) {
        int u, v;
        cin >> u >> v;
        adj[u].push_back(v);
        adj[v].push_back(u);
    }

    vector<int> cycle = find_cycle(N, adj);
    vector<pair<int, int>> cycle_edges;
    for (int i = 0; i < cycle.size(); ++i) {
        int u = cycle[i];
        int v = cycle[(i+1) % cycle.size()];
        cycle_edges.emplace_back(u, v);
    }

    int global_max = 0;

    for (auto& e : cycle_edges) {
        int a = e.first;
        int b = e.second;
        set<pair<int, int>> forbidden = {{a, b}, {b, a}};

        int u = bfs_farthest(0, adj, forbidden);
        u = bfs_farthest(u, adj, forbidden);
        int v_end = bfs_farthest(u, adj, forbidden);

        int sum_u = bfs_sum(u, adj, forbidden);
        int sum_v = bfs_sum(v_end, adj, forbidden);
        int current_max = max(sum_u, sum_v);

        if (current_max > global_max) {
            global_max = current_max;
        }
    }

    cout << global_max + N << endl;

    return 0;

\[
\left\{
\begin{matrix}
x^2(y+3) = 4(2-y) \\
y^2(z+3) = 4(2-z) \\
z^2(x+3) = 4(2-x)
\end{matrix}
\right.
\]

we first consider the possibility that \(x = y = z\). Substituting \(x = y = z\) into the first equation, we get:

\[
x^2(x + 3) = 4(2 - x)
\]

Expanding and simplifying:

\[
x^3 + 3x^2 = 8 - 4x \implies x^3 + 3x^2 + 4x - 8 = 0
\]

Testing possible rational roots, we find \(x = 1\) is a root. We factor the cubic polynomial using synthetic division:

\[
(x - 1)(x^2 + 4x + 8) = 0
\]

The quadratic factor \(x^2 + 4x + 8\) has complex roots, so the only real solution is \(x = 1\). Thus, \(x = y = z = 1\) is a solution. We verify this solution in the original equations:

\[
1^2(1 + 3) = 4(2 - 1) \implies 4 = 4
\]

Since the equations are cyclic and symmetric, this solution satisfies all three equations.

Next, we consider if there are other solutions where \(x\), \(y\), and \(z\) are not equal. We express each variable in terms of another using the equations:

\[
y = \frac{8 - 3x^2}{x^2 + 4}, \quad z = \frac{8 - 3y^2}{y^2 + 4}, \quad x = \frac{8 - 3z^2}{z^2 + 4}
\]

This leads to the function \(f(a) = \frac{8 - 3a^2}{a^2 + 4}\). We check for fixed points and cycles, but find that the only real fixed point is \(a = 1\). The derivative analysis shows the fixed point is unstable, suggesting no other real cycles exist.

Testing other potential values and cycles through substitution leads to inconsistencies or complex numbers, confirming no other real solutions exist.

Thus, the only real solution to the system is:

\[
\boxed{(1, 1, 1)}

def _recv_json(self):
    buffer = []
    while True:
        try:
            data = self.sock.recv(1)
        except BlockingIOError:
            break
        if not data:
            return None
        buffer.append(data)
        if data == b'\n':
            break
    if not buffer:
        return None

I implemented a basic strategy for the scraper bots that focuses on approaching enemies quickly and then transitioning to high firepower. The bots dynamically adjust their power allocation between movement, shields, and firepower based on the distance to their target.

**Step-by-Step Approach:**

1. **Establish Target:** Each bot selects the nearest enemy as its target every tick. Proximity is determined using Euclidean distance, ensuring efficient targeting.

2. **Movement Strategy:** 
   - When far away (distance > 600 du), bots allocate more power to movement (6 pu) to quickly close the gap. This speed helps reduce time spent in the ineffective long-range phase.
   - Once within 600 du, movement drops to 2 pu to conserve power for firepower and shields.

3. **Firepower Adjustment:** 
   - At distance, firepower is limited to 4 pu, spreading shots while focusing on reaching optimal engagement range.
   - When close (≤600 du), firepower is maximized at 8 pu for sustained, higher damage output.

4. **Shield Allocation:** 
   - A consistent 2 pu is allocated to shields throughout the engagement. This provides basic defense without sacrificing critical movement or firepower. The shield regeneration allows occasional protection against incoming attacks.

5. **Dynamic Targeting and Commands:** 
   - Bots continuously monitor enemy positions, re-evaluating closest threats and adjusting their target as needed.
   - Power and targeting commands are only updated when changes occur to reduce unnecessary communication, improving efficiency.

**Why This Works:**

- **Early Game:** Focusing on speed allows bots to minimize ineffective long-range combat and quickly enter the area where their attacks are more potent.
- **Engagement Phase:** Maximizing firepower at close range ensures targeted enemies receive heavy damage while the shield allocation provides enough protection to survive initial volleys.
- **Efficiency:** Dynamic power redistribution ensures resources are used where most effective at any given time, balancing aggression and survival.

The strategy prioritizes quick-win scenarios where bots can close rapidly and overwhelm targets, making it a straightforward yet deadly initial approach.

```python
import socket
import json
import math

class BotPlayer:
    def __init__(self):
        self.sock = socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_STREAM)
        self.sock.connect(('localhost', 50000))
        self.bots = {}
        self.my_pid = None
        self.my_bots = []
        self.target_cache = {}
        self.power_cache = {}
        self.sock.setblocking(0)

    def run(self):
        self._handle_ready()
        while True:
            self._process_messages()
            actions = self._calculate_actions()
            for action in actions:
                self._send_command(action)

    def _handle_ready(self):
        msg = self._recv_json()
        if msg['Type'] != 'READY':
            raise RuntimeError("Expected READY")
        self.my_pid = msg['PID']
        for bot in msg['Bots']:
            pid = bot['PID']
            bid = bot['BID']
            self.bots[(pid, bid)] = bot
            if pid == self.my_pid:
                self.my_bots.append(bid)
        self.power_cache = {bid: (4, 4, 4) for bid in self.my_bots}
        self.target_cache = {bid: (0, 0) for bid in self.my_bots}

    def _process_messages(self):
        while True:
            try:
                msg = self._recv_json()
                if not msg:
                    break
                if msg['Type'] == 'BOT':
                    self._update_bot(msg['PID'], msg['BID'], msg)
            except BlockingIOError:
                break

    def _update_bot(self, pid, bid, data):
        key = (pid, bid)
        current_bot = self.bots.get(key, {})
        current_bot.update(data)
        self.bots[key] = current_bot

    def _recv_json(self):
        chunks = []
        while True:
            try:
                chunk = self.sock.recv(1)
            except BlockingIOError:
                break
            if not chunk:
                return None
            if chunk == b'\n':
                break
            chunks.append(chunk)
        if not chunks:
            return None
        return json.loads(b''.join(chunks).decode('utf-8'))

    def _send_command(self, cmd):
        self.sock.sendall((json.dumps(cmd) + '\n').encode('utf-8'))

    def _calculate_actions(self):
        actions = []
        for bid in self.my_bots:
            bot_data = self.bots.get((self.my_pid, bid), None)
            if not bot_data or bot_data.get('Health', 0) <= 0:
                continue
            enemies = [b for (pid, b_id), b in self.bots.items()
                       if pid != self.my_pid and b.get("Health", 0) > 0]
            if not enemies:
                continue
            closest = self._find_closest(bot_data, enemies)
            curr_target = self.target_cache.get(bid, (0,0))
            new_target = (closest['PID'], closest['BID'])
            if new_target != curr_target:
                target_cmd = {
                    'Cmd': 'TARGET',
                    'BID': bid,
                    'TPID': closest['PID'],
                    'TBID': closest['BID']
                }
                actions.append(target_cmd)
                self.target_cache[bid] = new_target

            move_x, move_y = closest['X'], closest['Y']
            move_cmd = {
                'Cmd': 'MOVE',
                'BID': bid,
                'X': move_x,
                'Y': move_y
            }
            actions.append(move_cmd)

            dx = closest['X'] - bot_data['X']
            dy = closest['<#sep#>Search for all the products matching some criteria with a given provider
We have had a lot of discussions on a private GitLab instance before we moved to GitHub about how to search/download all the products matching some criteria.

The problems we observed with the current API were:
* The method `search` returns a tuple of `(SearchResult, int)`. The first item contains some products, in most cases 20 if the user hasn't defined `items_per_page` in the search (20 being the default value) or less than 20 if less products match the criteria. The second item is the total number of products matching the criteria, it is obtained after sending a count request (or equivalent) to the provider. This number may in some cases just be an estimate, usually lower than the correct value (this is true for peps at the time of writing). So what happens is that the user actually gets the results from the first page only, but it's not so easy to understand (https://github.com/CS-SI/eodag/issues/149)
* We have the method `download_all` that does what it says, it downloads all the products contained in the `SearchResult` it takes as an argument. However, some users would expect that it just downloads all the products matching the criteria that were used to search them in the first place.
* There is no clear way or example about how to search (and download) for all the products matching some criteria. The current way would be to execute a `search` by incrementing the page value given as a parameter. The issue with this approach is that it would each time send a count request (whether it's a proper request or not depends on the underlying search plugin implementation, but in most cases it's currently the case), while this doesn't seem necessary. Or by increasing `items_per_page` to a very high value, which may be too high (providers have their own limit) and result in potentially an obscure error.

We have discussed different approaches:
1. Add some superpower to `download_all` so that it could download more than the products it would be given. The idea was to store in the `SearchResult` all the information required to run the same search, and as a consequence to run the search on the next page. The next products found would then have been downloaded. And so on until exhaustion of the results. It would have been used as follows: `products, _ = dag.search(**criteria); paths = dag.download_all(products, exhaust=True)`
2. Add a `search_all` method that would collect all the products matching some criteria.
  2.1. By adding to `SearchResult` the possibility to trigger a search for the next page (same idea as with `download_all`)
  2.2. By adding a `search_iter_page` method to `EODataAccessGateway` that would iterate over the pages of a search, and using that method internally to implement `search_all`.

The approach *2.2.* was chosen to be implemented (which doesn't necessarily mean the other approaches can't or won't be implemented if they prove to be required):
* Just adding more power to `download_all` felt like it was not sufficient. Since some providers do not return the exact count of products available in a `search`, it would have been impossible for these providers to know beforehand how many products  `download_all` would download. This feels a a little inappropriate.
* `search_all` happens to mirror `download_all` quite well, the usage of `products = dag.search_all(**criteria); paths = dag.download_all(products)` feels quite natural
* Exposing a method `search_iter_page` will help us to document and explain that (most) searches are paginated. This is not obvious to every user while this is important to understand how eodag works.

#include <bits/stdc++.h>
using namespace std;

const int LOG = 20;

int main() {
    ios_base::sync_with_stdio(false);
    cin.tie(0);

    int n;
    cin >> n;
    vector<int> parent(n+1);
    for (int i=1; i<=n; ++i) {
        cin >> parent[i];
    }

    vector<vector<int>> children(n+1);
    for (int u=1; u<=n; ++u) {
        if (parent[u] != 0) {
            children[parent[u]].push_back(u);
        }
    }

    vector<int> in_time(n+1), out_time(n+1);
    vector<int> depth(n+1);
    vector<bool> visited(n+1, false);
    int timer = 0;

    for (int u=1; u<=n; ++u) {
        if (parent[u] == 0 && !visited[u]) {
            stack<pair<int, int>> st;
            st.push({u, -1});
            while (!st.empty()) {
                auto [v, pdepth] = st.top();
                if (visited[v]) {
                    out_time[v] = ++timer;
                    st.pop();
                    continue;
                }
                visited[v] = true;
                in_time[v] = ++timer;
                depth[v] = (pdepth == -1) ? 0 : pdepth + 1;
                auto& cs = children[v];
                for (auto it = cs.rbegin(); it != cs.rend(); ++it) {
                    int c = *it;
                    if (!visited[c]) {
                        st.push({c, depth[v]});
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    vector<vector<int>> depth_to_in;
    for (int u=1; u<=n; ++u) {
        if (depth_to_in.size() <= depth[u]) {
            depth_to_in.resize(depth[u] + 1);
        }
        depth_to_in[depth[u]].push_back(in_time[u]);
    }

    for (auto& vec : depth_to_in) {
        sort(vec.begin(), vec.end());
    }

    vector<vector<int>> lift(LOG, vector<int>(n+1, 0));
    for (int u=1; u<=n; ++u) {
        lift[0][u] = parent[u];
    }

    for (int k=1; k<LOG; ++k) {
        for (int u=1; u<=n; ++u) {
            int prev = lift[k-1][u];
            if (prev != 0) {
                lift[k][u] = lift[k-1][prev];
            } else {
                lift[k][u] = 0;
            }
        }
    }

    int m;
    cin >> m;
    while (m--) {
        int v, p;
        cin >> v >> p;

        int current = v;
        bool valid = true;
        for (int k=0; k<LOG; ++k) {
            if (p & (1 << k)) {
                current = lift[k][current];
                if (current == 0) {
                    valid = false;
                    break;
                }
            }
        }

        if (!valid) {
            cout << 0 << ' ';
            continue;
        }

        int z = current;
        if (depth[z] + p != depth[v]) {
            cout << 0 << ' ';
            continue;
        }

        int target_d = depth[v];
        if (target_d >= depth_to_in.size() || depth_to_in[target_d].empty()) {
            cout << 0 << ' ';
            continue;
        }

        const auto& lst = depth_to_in[target_d];
        int L = in_time[z], R = out_time[z];
        auto left = lower_bound(lst.begin(), lst.end(), L);
        auto right = upper_bound(lst.begin(), lst.end(), R);
        int cnt = right - left;
        cout << (cnt - 1) << ' ';
    }

    cout << endl;

    def _find_closest(self, bot_data, enemies):
        min_dist_sq = float('inf')
        closest = None
        ref_x, ref_y = bot_data['X'], bot_data['Y']
        for enemy in enemies:
            dx = enemy['X'] - ref_x
            dy = enemy['Y'] - ref_y
            dist_sq = dx**2 + dy**2
            if dist_sq < min_dist_sq:
                min_dist_sq = dist_sq
                closest = enemy
        return closest

+                      ``dict.fromkeys(["lonmin", "latmin", "lonmax", "latmax"])``
+                    * with a bounding box as list of float:
+                      ``[lonmin, latmin, lonmax, latmax]``
+                    * with a WKT str
+
+        :type geom: Union[str, dict, shapely.geometry.base.BaseGeometry]
+        :param locations: Location filtering by name using locations configuration
+                          ``{"<location_name>"="<attr_regex>"}``. For example, ``{"country"="PA."}`` will use
+                          the geometry of the features having the property ISO3 starting with
+                          'PA' such as Panama and Pakistan in the shapefile configured with
+                          name=country and attr=ISO3
+        :type locations: dict
+        :param dict kwargs: some other criteria that will be used to do the search,
+                            using paramaters compatibles with the provider
+        :returns: An iterator that yields page per page a collection of EO products
+                  matching the criteria
+        :rtype: Iterator[:class:`~eodag.api.search_result.SearchResult`]
+
+        .. versionadded::
+            2.2.0
+        """
+        search_kwargs = self._prepare_search(
+            start=start, end=end, geom=geom, locations=locations, **kwargs
+        )
+        search_plugin = search_kwargs.pop("search_plugin")
+        iteration = 1
+        # Store the search plugin config pagination.next_page_url_tpl to reset it later
+        # since it might be modified if the next_page_url mechanism is used by the
+        # plugin.
+        pagination_config = getattr(search_plugin.config, "pagination", {})
+        prev_next_page_url_tpl = pagination_config.get("next_page_url_tpl")
+        # Page has to be set to a value even if use_next is True, this is required
+        # internally by the search plugin (see collect_search_urls)
+        search_kwargs.update(
+            page=1,
+            items_per_page=items_per_page,
+        )
+        prev_product = None
+        next_page_url = None
+        while True:
+            if iteration > 1 and next_page_url:
+                pagination_config["next_page_url_tpl"] = next_page_url
+            logger.debug("Iterate over pages: search page %s", iteration)
+            try:
+                products, _ = self._do_search(
+                    search_plugin, count=False, raise_errors=True, **search_kwargs
+                )
+            finally:
+                # we don't want that next(search_iter_page(...)) modifies the plugin
+                # indefinitely. So we reset after each request, but before the generator
+                # yields, the attr next_page_url (to None) and
+                # config.pagination["next_page_url_tpl"] (to its original value).
+                next_page_url = getattr(search_plugin, "next_page_url", None)
+                if next_page_url:
+                    search_plugin.next_page_url = None
+                    if prev_next_page_url_tpl:
+                        search_plugin.config.pagination[
+                            "next_page_url_tpl"
+                        ] = prev_next_page_url_tpl
+            if len(products) > 0:
+                # The first products between two iterations are compared. If they
+                # are actually the same product, it means the iteration failed at
+                # progressing for some reason. This is implemented as a workaround
+                # to some search plugins/providers not handling pagination.
+                product = products[0]
+                if (
+                    prev_product
+                    and product.properties["id"] == prev_product.properties["id"]
+                    and product.provider == prev_product.provider
+                ):
+                    logger.warning(
+                        "Iterate over pages: stop iterating since the next page "
+                        "appears to have the same products as in the previous one. "
+                        "This provider may not implement pagination.",
+                    )
+                    last_page_with_products = iteration - 1
+                    break
+                yield products
+                prev_product = product
+                # Prevent a last search if the current one returned less than the
+                # maximum number of items asked for.
+                if len(products) < items_per_page:
+                    last_page_with_products = iteration
+                    break
+            else:
+                last_page_with_products = iteration - 1
+                break
+            iteration += 1
+            search_kwargs["page"] = iteration
+        logger.debug(
+            "Iterate over pages: last products found on page %s",
+            last_page_with_products,
+        )
+
+    def search_all(
+        self,
+        items_per_page=None,
+        start=None,
+        end=None,
+        geom=None,
+        locations=None,
+        **kwargs,
+    ):
+        """Search and return all the products matching the search criteria.
+
+        It iterates over the pages of a search query and collects all the returned
+        products into a single :class:`~eodag.api.search_result.SearchResult` instance.
+
+        :param items_per_page: (optional) The number of results requested internally per
+                               page. The maximum number of items than can be requested
+                               at once to a provider has been configured in EODAG for
+                               some of them. If items_per_page is None and this number
+                               is available for the searched provider, it is used to
+                               limit the number of requests made. This should also
+                               reduce the time required to collect all the products
+                               matching the search criteria. If this number is not
+                               available, a default value of 50 is used instead.
+                               items_per_page can also be set to any arbitrary value.
+        :type items_per_page: int
+        :param start: Start sensing UTC time in iso format
+        :type start: str
+        :param end: End sensing UTC time in iso format
+        :type end: str
+        :param geom: Search area that can be defined in different ways:
+
+                    * with a Shapely geometry object:
+                      ``class:`shapely.geometry.base.BaseGeometry```
+                    * with a bounding box (dict with keys: "lonmin", "latmin", "lonmax", "latmax"):
+                      ``dict.fromkeys(["lonmin", "latmin", "lonmax", "latmax"])``
+                    * with a bounding box as list of float:
+                      ``[lonmin, latmin, lonmax, latmax]``
+                    * with a WKT str
+
+        :type geom: Union[str, dict, shapely.geometry.base.BaseGeometry]
+        :param locations: Location filtering by name using locations configuration
+                          ``{"<location_name>"="<attr_regex>"}``. For example, ``{"country"="PA."}`` will use
+                          the geometry of the features having the property ISO3 starting with
+                          'PA' such as Panama and Pakistan in the shapefile configured with
+                          name=country and attr=ISO3
+        :type locations: dict
+        :param dict kwargs: some other criteria that will be used to do the search,
+                            using paramaters compatibles with the provider
+        :returns: An iterator that yields page per page a collection of EO products
+                  matching the criteria
+        :rtype: Iterator[:class:`~eodag.api.search_result.SearchResult`]
+
+        .. versionadded::
+            2.2.0
+        """
+        # Prepare the search just to get the search plugin and the maximized value
+        # of items_per_page if defined for the provider used.
+        search_kwargs = self._prepare_search(
+            start=start, end=end, geom=geom, locations=locations, **kwargs
+        )
+        search_plugin = search_kwargs["search_plugin"]
+        if items_per_page is None:
+            items_per_page = search_plugin.config.pagination.get(
+                "max_items_per_page", DEFAULT_MAX_ITEMS_PER_PAGE
+            )
+        logger.debug(
+            "Searching for all the products with provider %s and a maximum of %s "
+            "items per page.",
+            search_plugin.provider,
+            items_per_page,
+        )
+        all_results = SearchResult([])
+        for page_results in self.search_iter_page(
+            items_per_page=items_per_page,
+            start=start,
+            end=end,
+            geom=geom,
+            locations=locations,
+            **kwargs,
+        ):
+            all_results.data.extend(page_results.data)
+        logger.info(
+            "Found %s result(s) on provider '%s'",
+           <#sep#>/m2 )
